are:

\displaystyle J^{\pi^{E}}(U_{1};p^{\prime},r)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}U_{1}(0)+\frac{1}{4}U_{1}(0.25)+\frac{1}{4}U_{1}(0.75)+\frac{1}{4}U_{1}(1)=2.02/4=0.505,

\displaystyle J^{\overline{\pi}}(U_{1};p^{\prime},r)

\displaystyle=0.7U_{1}(0)+0.3U_{1}(1)=0.3\times 1.99=0.597,

\displaystyle J^{\pi^{E}}(U_{2};p^{\prime},r)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}U_{1}(0)+\frac{1}{4}U_{1}(0.25)+\frac{1}{4}U_{1}(0.75)+\frac{1}{4}U_{1}(1)=2.99/4=0.7475,

\displaystyle J^{\overline{\pi}}(U_{2};p^{\prime},r)

\displaystyle=0.7U_{1}(0)+0.3U_{1}(1)=0.3\times 1.99=0.597.

Clearly,
J^{\pi^{E}}(U_{1};p^{\prime},r)<J^{\overline{\pi}}(U_{1};p^{\prime},r)
, but

J^{\pi^{E}}(U_{2};p^{\prime},r)>J^{\overline{\pi}}(U_{2};p^{\prime},r)
, thus we conclude that the
set of policies induced by utilities
U_{1},U_{2}
in
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
do not intersect,
since they start from
s_{\text{init}}
with different actions

\Pi^{*}_{p^{\prime},r}(U_{1})\cap\Pi^{*}_{p^{\prime},r}(U_{2})=\{\}
. This concludes the proof
with an example that satisfies
\mathcal{G}^{p^{\prime},r}=\mathcal{G}^{p,r}
.

If we want an example that does
not
satisfy
\mathcal{G}^{p^{\prime},r}=\mathcal{G}^{p,r}
,
then we can consider exactly the same example with
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
, but
using
r_{1}(s_{\text{init}},a_{2})=0.001
. In this manner, we see that

\mathcal{G}^{p,r}=\{0,0.25,0.251,0.75,0.751,1\}
, and

\mathcal{G}^{p^{\prime},r}=\{0,0.001,0.25,0.75,1,1.001\}
, which are different. By choosing

U_{1}^{\prime},U_{2}^{\prime}
as:

\displaystyle U_{1}^{\prime}(G)=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }G=0\\
0.001&\text{if }G=0.001\\
0.01&\text{if }G=0.25\\
0.011&\text{if }G=0.251\\
0.02&\text{if }G=0.75\\
0.021&\text{if }G=0.751\\
1.99&\text{if }G=1\\
1.991&\text{if }G=1.001\end{cases},\qquad U_{2}^{\prime}(G)=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }G=0\\
0.001&\text{if }G=0.001\\
0.01&\text{if }G=0.25\\
0.011&\text{if }G=0.251\\
0.99&\text{if }G=0.75\\
0.991&\text{if }G=0.751\\
1.99&\text{if }G=1\\
1.991&\text{if }G=1.001\end{cases},

it can be shown that
U_{1}^{\prime},U_{2}^{\prime}
belong to the (new) feasible set of
\mathcal{M}
,
and that induce different policies in
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

See
4.2

Proof.

Similarly to the proof of Proposition

,

Figure 7:

.

Now, we show that the utilities

, defined in points of the support
\mathcal{G}^{p,r}
as (and connected in
arbitrary continuous strictly-increasing manner between these points):

\displaystyle U_{1}(G)=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }G=0\\
0.1&\text{if }G=0.5\\
0.9&\text{if }G=1\\
1.5&\text{if }G=1.5\end{cases},\qquad U_{2}(G)=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }G=0\\
0.1&\text{if }G=0.5\\
0.8&\text{if }G=1\\
1.5&\text{if }G=1.5\end{cases},

belong to the feasible set
\mathcal{U}_{p,r,\pi^{E}}
, and, when transferred to the new
MDP
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},H,s_{\text{init}},p,r^{\prime})
, with
reward function
r^{\prime}\neq r
defined as:

\displaystyle r_{1}^{\prime}(s_{\text{init}},a_{1})=0.5,\qquad r_{1}(s_{\text{init}},a_{2})=0,

\displaystyle r_{2}^{\prime}(s,a)=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }s=s_{1}\wedge(a=a_{1}\lor a=a_{2})\\
0&\text{if }s=s_{2}\wedge(a=a_{1}\lor a=a_{2})\end{cases},

impose different optimal policies, i.e., utility
U_{2}
keeps making action

a_{1}
optimal from state
s_{\text{init}}
even in
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
, while
U_{1}
makes
action
a_{2}
optimal. This will demonstrate the thesis of the proposition.

Let us begin by showing that
U_{1},U_{2}\in\mathcal{U}_{p,r,\pi^{E}}
belong to the feasible
set of
\mathcal{M}
with policy
\pi^{E}
. Let
\overline{\pi}
be the policy that
plays action
a_{2}
in state
s_{\text{init}}
. Then, the distribution of
returns induced by policies
\pi^{E}
and
\overline{\pi}
are (we represent
values only at points in
\mathcal{G}^{p,r}=\{0,0.5,1,1.5\}
):

\displaystyle\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}

\displaystyle=[0.5,0,0.5,0]^{\intercal}

\displaystyle\eta^{p,r,\overline{\pi}}

\displaystyle=[0,0.9,0,0.1]^{\intercal}.

Thus, policy
\pi^{E}
is optimal under some utility
U
if and only if the
values assigned by
U
to points in
\mathcal{G}^{p,r}=\{0,0.5,1,1.5\}
(denoted,
respectively, by
U^{1},U^{2},U^{3},U^{4}
) satisfy:

\displaystyle U^{\intercal}(\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}-\eta^{p,r,\overline{\pi}})=[0.5,-0.9,0.5,-0.1]U=0.5U^{1}-0.9U^{2}+0.5U^{3}-0.1U^{4}\geq 0,

where we have overloaded the notation and denoted with

U\coloneqq[U^{1},U^{2},U^{3},U^{4}]^{\intercal}
both the utility and the vector of
values assigned to points in
\mathcal{G}^{p,r}
.
By imposing normalization constraints (
U(0)=0,U(2)=2
), we get
U^{1}=0
, and
by imposing also the monotonicity constraints, we get that utility
U
is in
the feasible set
\mathcal{U}_{p,r,\pi^{E}}
if and only if:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}U^{4}\geq 5U^{3}-9U^{2}\\
0<U^{2}<U^{3}<U^{4}<2\end{cases}.

Clearly, both utilities
U_{1},U_{2}
satisfy these constraints, thus they
belong to the feasible set
\mathcal{U}_{p,r,\pi^{E}}
.
Now, concerning problem
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
, the performances of
\pi^{E},\overline{\pi}

w.r.t. utilities
U_{1},U_{2}
are:

\displaystyle J^{\pi^{E}}(U_{1};p,r^{\prime})

\displaystyle=0U_{1}(0)+0.5U_{1}(0.5)+0U_{1}(1)+0.5U_{1}(1.5)=1.6/2=0.8,

\displaystyle J^{\overline{\pi}}(U_{1};p,r^{\prime})

\displaystyle=0.1U_{1}(0)+0U_{1}(0.5)+0.9U_{1}(1)+0U_{1}(1.5)=0.9\times 0.9=0.81,

\displaystyle J^{\pi^{E}}(U_{2};p,r^{\prime})

\displaystyle=0U_{2}(0)+0.5U_{2}(0.5)+0U_{2}(1)+0.5U_{2}(1.5)=1.6/2=0.8,

\displaystyle J^{\overline{\pi}}(U_{2};p,r^{\prime})

\displaystyle=0.1U_{2}(0)+0U_{2}(0.5)+0.9U_{2}(1)+0U_{2}(1.5)=0.9\times 0.8=0.72.

Clearly,
J^{\pi^{E}}(U_{1};p,r^{\prime})<J^{\overline{\pi}}(U_{1};p,r^{\prime})
, but

J^{\pi^{E}}(U_{2};p,r^{\prime})>J^{\overline{\pi}}(U_{2};p,r^{\prime})
, thus we conclude that the
set of policies induced by utilities
U_{1},U_{2}
in
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
do not intersect,
since they start from
s_{\text{init}}
with different actions

\Pi^{*}_{p,r^{\prime}}(U_{1})\cap\Pi^{*}_{p,r^{\prime}}(U_{2})=\{\}
. This concludes the proof
with an example that satisfies
\mathcal{G}^{p,r^{\prime}}=\mathcal{G}^{p,r}
.

If we want an example that does
not
satisfy
\mathcal{G}^{p,r^{\prime}}=\mathcal{G}^{p,r}
,
then we can consider exactly the same example with
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
, but
using
r_{1}^{\prime}(s_{\text{init}},a_{2})=0.001
. In this manner, we see that

\mathcal{G}^{p,r}=\{0,0.5,1,1.5\}
, and
\mathcal{G}^{p^{\prime},r}=\{0.001,0.5,1.001,1.5\}
, which
are different. Nevertheless, by choosing
U_{1}^{\prime},U_{2}^{\prime}
as:

\displaystyle U_{1}^{\prime}(G)=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }G=0\\
0.001&\text{if }G=0.001\\
0.1&\text{if }G=0.5\\
0.9&\text{if }G=1\\
0.901&\text{if }G=1.001\\
1.5&\text{if }G=1.5\end{cases},\qquad U_{2}^{\prime}(G)=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }G=0\\
0.001&\text{if }G=0.001\\
0.1&\text{if }G=0.5\\
0.8&\text{if }G=1\\
0.801&\text{if }G=1.001\\
1.5&\text{if }G=1.5\end{cases},

it can be shown that
U_{1}^{\prime},U_{2}^{\prime}
still belong to the feasible set of
\mathcal{M}

(the constraints are the same), and that induce different policies in

\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

See
4.3

Proof.

Consider a simple MDP

\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},H,s_{\text{init}},p,r)
with four states

\mathcal{S}=\{s_{\text{init}},s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}\}
, three actions

\mathcal{A}=\{a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\}
, horizon
H=2
, initial state
s_{\text{init}}
,
transition model
p
such that:

\displaystyle p_{1}(s_{2}|s_{\text{init}},a_{1})=1,\qquad p_{1}(s_{1}|s_{\text{init}},a_{3})=1,

\displaystyle p_{1}(s^{\prime}|s_{\text{init}},a_{2})=\begin{cases}0.91&\text{if }s^{\prime}=s_{1}\\
0.09&\text{if }s^{\prime}=s_{3}\end{cases},

and reward function
r
that assigns

r_{1}(s_{\text{init}},a_{1})=r_{1}(s_{\text{init}},a_{2})=r_{1}(s_{\text{init}},a_{3})=0
, and:

\displaystyle r_{2}(s,a)=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }s=s_{1}\wedge(a=a_{1}\lor a=a_{2}\lor a=a_{3})\\
0.5&\text{if }s=s_{2}\wedge(a=a_{1}\lor a=a_{2}\lor a=a_{3})\\
1&\text{if }s=s_{3}\wedge(a=a_{1}\lor a=a_{2}\lor a=a_{3})\end{cases}.

Note that the support of the return function is
\mathcal{G}^{p,r}=\{0,0.5,1\}
. We
are given an expert’s policy
\pi^{E}
that prescribes action
a_{1}
at stage 1
in state
s_{\text{init}}
, and arbitrary actions in other states (the
specific action is not relevant). The MDP

\mathcal{M}
is represented in Figure
8
.

s_{\text{init}}
start
s_{1}
s_{2}
s_{3}
a_{1}
a_{3}
a_{2}
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
0.91
0.09

Figure 8:
MDP for the proof of Proposition
4.3
.

Now, we show that the utilities

U_{1},U_{2}\in\mathfrak{U}
, defined in points of the support
\mathcal{G}^{p,r}
as (and connected in
arbitrary continuous strictly-increasing manner between these points):

\displaystyle U_{1}(G)=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }G=0\\
0.1&\text{if }G=0.5\\
0.1/0.09&\text{if }G=1\end{cases},\qquad U_{2}(G)=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }G=0\\
1.099&\text{if }G=0.5\\
1.1&\text{if }G=1\end{cases},

belong to the feasible set
\mathcal{U}_{p,r,\pi^{E}}
, and that, for any

\epsilon\in[0,0.1]
, there exists a policy
\pi
for which it holds both
that
J^{*}(U_{1};p,r)-J^{\pi}(U_{1};p,r)=\epsilon
and

J^{*}(U_{2};p,r)-J^{\pi}(U_{2};p,r)\geq 1
.

First, let us show that both
U_{1},U_{2}
belong to the feasible utility set.
Let
\pi^{1},\pi^{2},\pi^{3}
be the policies that play, respectively, action

a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
in state
s_{\text{init}}
(note that
\pi^{1}=\pi^{E}
). Then,
their performances for arbitrary utility
U
are:

\displaystyle J^{\pi^{1}}(U;p,r)=U(0.5),

\displaystyle J^{\pi^{2}}(U;p,r)=0.09U(1)+0.91U(0)=0.09U(1),

\displaystyle J^{\pi^{3}}(U;p,r)=U(0)=0,

where we have used the normalization condition. Replacing
U
with
U_{1}
, we
get
J^{*}(U_{1};p,r)=J^{\pi^{1}}(U_{1};p,r)=0.1{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}=}J^{\pi^{2}}(U_{1};p,r)=0.1>J^{\pi^{3}}(U_{1};p,r)=0
. Instead, replacing with
U_{2}
, we get
J^{*}(U_{2};p,r)=J^{\pi^{1}}(U_{2};p,r)=1.099>J^{\pi^{2}}(U_{2};p,r)=0.09\times 1.1>J^{\pi^{3}}(U_{2};p,r)=0
. Therefore, both

U_{1},U_{2}\in\mathcal{U}_{p,r,\pi^{E}}
.

Now, for any
\alpha\in[0,1]
let us denote by
\pi_{\alpha}
the policy that,
at state
s_{\text{init}}
, plays action
a_{3}
w.p.
\alpha
, and action

a_{2}
w.p.
1-\alpha
. We show that, for any
\epsilon\in[0,0.1]
, policy

\pi_{\epsilon/0.1}
is
\epsilon
-optimal for utility
U_{1}
, and its
suboptimality is at least
1
under utility
U_{2}
. For any
\alpha\in[0,1]
,
the expected utilities of policy
\pi_{\alpha}
under
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
are:

\displaystyle J^{\pi_{\alpha}}(U_{1};p,r)=(1-\alpha)\times 0.09\times U_{1}(1)=(1-\alpha)\times 0.1,

\displaystyle J^{\pi_{\alpha}}(U_{2};p,r)=(1-\alpha)\times 0.09\times U_{2}(1)=(1-\alpha)\times 0.099,

from which we derive that the suboptimalities of such policy under
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
are:

\displaystyle J^{*}(U_{1};p,r)-J^{\pi_{\alpha}}(U_{1};p,r)=0.1-(1-\alpha)\times 0.1=0.1\alpha,

\displaystyle J^{*}(U_{2};p,r)-J^{\pi_{\alpha}}(U_{2};p,r)=1.099-(1-\alpha)\times 0.099=1+0.099\alpha.

Thus, for any
\epsilon\in[0,0.1]
, policy
\pi_{\epsilon/0.1}
is

\epsilon
-optimal for utility
U_{1}
, but it is at least
1
-suboptimal for
utility
U_{2}
.

The intuition is that utilities
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
assess in completely
different manners the policies that play action
a_{2}
, although they both
describe policy
\pi^{E}
as optimal. This concludes the proof.
∎

Proof.

Consider a simple MDP

\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},H,s_{\text{init}},p,r)
with three states

\mathcal{S}=\{s_{\text{init}},s_{1},s_{2}\}
, three actions

\mathcal{A}=\{a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\}
, horizon
H=2
, initial state
s_{\text{init}}
,
transition model
p
such that:

\displaystyle p_{1}(s_{1}|s_{\text{init}},a_{1})=1,\qquad p_{1}(s_{2}|s_{\text{init}},a_{2})=p_{1}(s_{2}|s_{\text{init}},a_{2})=1,

and reward function
r
that assigns

r_{1}(s_{\text{init}},a_{1})=r_{1}(s_{\text{init}},a_{2})=0
,
r_{1}(s_{\text{init}},a_{2})=1
, and:

\displaystyle r_{2}(s,a)=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }s=s_{1}\wedge(a=a_{1}\lor a=a_{2}\lor a_{3})\\
1&\text{if }s=s_{2}\wedge(a=a_{1}\lor a=a_{2}\lor a_{3})\end{cases}.

Note that the support of the return function is
\mathcal{G}^{p,r}=\{0,1,2\}
. We
are given an expert’s policy
\pi^{E}
that prescribes action
a_{3}
at stage 1
in state
s_{\text{init}}
, and arbitrary actions in the other states (the
specific action is not relevant). The MDP

\mathcal{M}
is represented in Figure
9
.

s_{\text{init}}
start
s_{1}
s_{2}
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
a_{1}
a_{2},a_{3}

Figure 9:
MDP for the proof of Proposition
4.4
.

Consider two utilities
U_{1},U_{2}
, that take on the following values in

\mathcal{G}^{p,r}
:

\displaystyle U_{1}(G)=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }G=0\\
0.1&\text{if }G=1\\
2&\text{if }G=2\end{cases},

\displaystyle U_{2}(G)=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }G=0\\
1.1&\text{if }G=1\\
2&\text{if }G=2\end{cases}.

It is immediate that both utilities belong to the feasible set

\mathcal{U}_{p,r,\pi^{E}}
. Nevertheless, if we denote by
\overline{\pi}
the policy

in state
s_{\text{init}}
, we see that

J^{\overline{\pi}}(U_{1};p,r)=0.1
, while
J^{\overline{\pi}}(U_{2};p,r)=1.1
,
so that the difference is
1
.

See
4.5

Proof.

We provide a constructive proof that shows which values of
s_{0},p,r
it is
sufficient to choose for recovering
U^{E}
exactly. The construction is
articulated into two parts. First, we aim to recover the value of
U^{E}(1)
,
i.e., for
G=1
; next, we recover the utility for all other possible values
of return. The intuition is that we construct a Standard Gamble (SG) between
two policies over the entire horizon
(
Wakker, 2010
)
.

To infer
U^{E}(1)
, we use the
s_{0},p,r
values that provide the MDP
described in Figure
10
.

s_{\text{init}}
start
s_{1}^{2}
s_{2}^{2}
s_{3}^{2}
s_{1}^{3}
s_{2}^{3}
s_{3}^{3}
s_{1}^{H}
s_{2}^{H}
s_{3}^{H}
\dotsc
\dotsc
\dotsc
h=1
h=2
h=3
\dotsc
h=H
a_{1}
a_{2}
1-q
q

Figure 10:
MDP for the proof of Proposition
4.5
.

We consider a single initial state
s_{\text{init}}
. From here, action

a_{1}
(and all actions other than
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
) brings deterministically
to state
s_{1}^{2}
, while action
a_{2}
brings to state
s_{3}^{2}
w.p.
q
(to
choose, for some
q\in[0,1]
), and to state
s_{2}^{2}
w.p.
1-q
. From state

s_{i}^{2}
, for any
i\in\llbracket 3\rrbracket
, all actions bring deterministically to state

s_{i}^{3}
, and so on, up to state
s_{i}^{H}
. We will call the trajectory

\{s_{\text{init}},s_{i}^{2},s_{i}^{3},\dotsc,s_{i}^{H}\}
the
i^{\text{th}}

trajectory for all
i\in\llbracket 3\rrbracket
, and we will write
G(i)
to denote the sum
of rewards along such trajectory. To infer the value
U^{E}(1)
, we select a
reward
r^{\prime}:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\llbracket H\rrbracket\to[0,1]
that provides return
G(1)=1.5
to the first
trajectory, return
G(2)=1
to the second trajectory, and return
G(3)=H
to
the third trajectory (this is possible because
H\geq 2
). By selecting,
successively, all the values of
q\in[0,1]
, we are asking to the expert to
play either action
a_{1}
or action
a_{2}
from the initial state

s_{\text{init}}
(we denote policies
\pi^{1},\pi^{2}
, respectively, the
policies that play actions
a_{1},a_{2}
in
s_{\text{init}}
). Since we are
assuming that the expert will demonstrate all the possible deterministic
optimal policies, there exists a value
q^{\prime}\in[0,1]
for which the expert
demonstrates both policies
\pi^{1}
and
\pi^{2}
. Indeed, the expected
utilities of policies
\pi^{1},\pi^{2}
for arbitrary value of
q
are (we write

p(q)
as the generic transition model):

\displaystyle\begin{split}&J^{\pi^{1}}(U^{E};p(q),r^{\prime})=U^{E}(1.5),\\
&J^{\pi^{2}}(U^{E};p(q),r^{\prime})=qU^{E}(H)+(1-q)U^{E}(1)=qH+(1-q)U^{E}(1),\end{split}

and since
U^{E}
is strictly-increasing, we have
U^{E}(1)<U^{E}(1.5)<U^{E}(H)=H
,
thus there must exist
q^{\prime}
that permits to write
U^{E}(1.5)
as a convex
combination of the other two. This allows us to write:

\displaystyle U^{E}(1.5)=q^{\prime}H+(1-q^{\prime})U^{E}(1).

(11)

Next, we select reward
r^{\prime\prime}
that provides returns

G(1)=1,G(2)=0.5,G(3)=1.5
. Thus, there must exist a
q^{\prime\prime}\in[0,1]
for which
the expert demonstrates both policies
\pi^{1}
and
\pi^{2}
, allowing us to
write:

\displaystyle U^{E}(1)=q^{\prime\prime}U^{E}(1.5)+(1-q^{\prime\prime})U^{E}(0.5).

(12)

Finally, we can repeat the same step with a third reward
r^{\prime\prime\prime}
that
provides returns
G(1)=0.5,G(2)=0,G(3)=1
, and for some
q^{\prime\prime\prime}\in[0,1]
we
obtain:

\displaystyle U^{E}(0.5)=q^{\prime\prime\prime}U^{E}(1).

(13)

By putting together Eq. (
11
), Eq. (
12
), and Eq. (
13
),
we can retrieve
U^{E}(1)
:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}U^{E}(1.5)=q^{\prime}H+(1-q^{\prime})U^{E}(1)\\
U^{E}(1)=q^{\prime\prime}U^{E}(1.5)+(1-q^{\prime\prime})U^{E}(0.5)\\
U^{E}(0.5)=q^{\prime\prime\prime}U^{E}(1)\end{cases}.

Now that we know
U^{E}(1)
, we can infer the utility for all the returns

\overline{G}\in(1,H)
by choosing a reward that provides returns

G(1)=\overline{G},G(2)=1,G(3)=H
, because for some
\overline{q}\in[0,1]

the expert will play both policies
\pi^{1}
and
\pi^{2}
, which allows us to
write:

\displaystyle U^{E}(\overline{G})=\overline{q}H+(1-\overline{q})U^{E}(1),

and to retrieve
U^{E}(\overline{G})
.

Similarly, for all
\overline{G}\in(0,1)
, we select a reward that provides
returns
G(1)=\overline{G},G(2)=0,G(3)=1
, and for some

\overline{q}\in[0,1]
we can write:

\displaystyle U^{E}(\overline{G})=\overline{q}U^{E}(1),

and retrieve
U^{E}(\overline{G})
.

This concludes the proof. As a final remark, we stress that the initial step
for inferring
U^{E}(1)
cannot be dropped because there is no reward

r:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\llbracket H\rrbracket\to[0,1]
that provides returns
G(2)=0
and
G(3)=H
, because both
the first and second trajectories pass through action
a_{2}
in state

s_{\text{init}}
.
∎

Appendix E
Additional Results and Proofs for Section
5

This appendix is divided in 5 parts. First, we show the complexity of
implementing operator
\Pi_{\overline{\underline{\mathfrak{U}}}_{L}}
(Appendix
E.1
). In Appendix
E.2
, we provide the pseudocode, along with a description, of
algorithms
EXPLORE
,
PLANNING
,
ERD
, and

ROLLOUT
. In Appendix
E.3
we analyze the time
and space complexities of
CATY-UL
and
TRACTOR-UL
. In Appendix
E.4
, we provide the proof of Theorem
5.1
. In
Appendix
E.5
, we provide the proof of Theorem
5.2
.

E.1
Projecting onto the Set of Discretized Utilities

Let us use the square brackets
[]
to denote the components of vectors. Then,
note that set
\overline{\underline{\mathfrak{U}}}_{L}
can be represented more explicitly as:

\displaystyle\overline{\underline{\mathfrak{U}}}_{L}=\{\overline{U}\in[0,H]^{d}\,|\,

\displaystyle\overline{U}[1]=0\wedge\overline{U}[d]=H\wedge\overline{U}[i]\leq\overline{U}[i+1]\;\forall i\in\llbracket d-1\rrbracket

\displaystyle\wedge\;\forall i,j\in\llbracket d\rrbracket\text{ s.t. }i<j:\,|\overline{U}[i]-\overline{U}[j]|\leq L(j-i)\epsilon_{0}\}.

(14)

Notice that set
\overline{\underline{\mathfrak{U}}}_{L}
is closed and convex, since it is defined by linear
constraints only.
The amount of constraints scales as
\propto d^{2}
.

We remark that in Theorem
5.2
we assume availability
of an oracle for computing the projection exactly. In practice, we can use any
quadratic programming solver to approximate the projection.

E.2
Missing Algorithms and Sub-routines

EXPLORE

In Algorithm
19
, we report
the pseudo-code implementing subroutine
EXPLORE
. Simply put, we adopt a
uniform-sampling strategy, i.e., we collect
n=\lfloor\tau/(SAH)\rfloor
samples from
each
(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\llbracket H\rrbracket
triple, that we use to compute the empirical estimate of
the transition model. We return such estimate.

\in

for

for

\_

\in

21

24

25

PLANNING

The
PLANNING
sub-routine (Algorithm

4
) takes in input a utility
U
, an environment index
i
, and
a transition model
p
, that uses to construct the RS-MDP

\mathcal{M}_{U}\coloneqq(\mathcal{S}^{i},\mathcal{A}^{i},H,s_{0}^{i},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\overline{r}^{i}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}U})
.
Notice that
\mathcal{M}_{U}\neq\mathcal{M}^{i}_{U^{E}}
, for 3 aspects. First, it uses the input
transition model
p\neq p^{i}
; next, it consider the discretized reward

\overline{r}^{i}\neq r^{i}
; finally, it has input utility
U\neq U^{E}
.

PLANNING
outputs two items. The optimal performance
J^{*}(U;p,r^{i})
for
RS-MDP
\mathcal{M}_{U}
, and the optimal policy
\psi^{*}=\{\psi^{*}_{h}\}_{h}
for the enlarged
state space MDP
\mathfrak{E}[\mathcal{M}_{U}]
. However, it should be remarked that, instead of
computing optimal policy
\psi^{*}
for
\mathfrak{E}[\mathcal{M}_{U}]
only at pairs

(s,y)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{G}^{p,\overline{r}^{i}}_{h}
for all
h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket
,

PLANNING
computes the optimal policy
\psi^{*}
at all pairs

(s,y)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h}
for all
h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket
(note that

\mathcal{G}^{p,\overline{r}^{i}}_{h}\subseteq\mathcal{Y}_{h}
).

The algorithm implemented in
PLANNING
for computing both
J^{*}(U;p,r^{i})

and
\psi^{*}
is value iteration. The difference from common implementations of
value iterations lies in the presence of an additional variable in the state. A
similar pseudocode is provided in Algorithm 1 of
Wu & Xu (2023)
.

Algorithm 4

PLANNING

Input :

utility
U
, environment index
i
, transition model
p

// Initialize the
Q
and value function at the last stage:

for

(s,y)\in\mathcal{S}^{i}\times\mathcal{Y}_{H}

do

for

a\in\mathcal{A}^{i}

do

29

Q_{H}(s,y,a)\leftarrow U(y+\overline{r}^{i}_{H}(s,a))

30

end for

33

31

V_{H}(s,y)\leftarrow\max\limits_{a\in\mathcal{A}^{i}}Q_{H}(s,y,a)

32

\psi_{H}(s,y)\leftarrow\argmax\limits_{a\in\mathcal{A}^{i}}Q_{H}(s,y,a)

/*
Keep just one action */

34

end for

// Backward induction:

for

h=H-1,\dotsc,2,1

do

for

(s,y)\in\mathcal{S}^{i}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h}

do

for

a\in\mathcal{A}^{i}

do

35

Q_{h}(s,y,a)\leftarrow\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim p_{h}(\cdot|s,a)}\Big[V_{h+1}(s^{\prime},y+\overline{r}^{i}_{h}(s,a))\Big]

36

end for

39

37

V_{h}(s,y)\leftarrow\max\limits_{a\in\mathcal{A}^{i}}Q_{h}(s,y,a)

38

\psi_{h}(s,y)\leftarrow\argmax\limits_{a\in\mathcal{A}^{i}}Q_{h}(s,y,a)

/*
Keep just one action */

40

end for

42

end for

// Return optimal performance and policy:

Return

V_{1}(s_{0}^{i},0),\psi

ERD
(Estimate the Return Distribution)

The
ERD
sub-routine (Algorithm
5
) takes in input a dataset

\mathcal{D}^{E}=\{\omega_{j}\}_{j}
of state-action trajectories
\omega_{j}\in\Omega
and a
reward function
r
, and it computes an estimate of the return distribution w.r.t.

r
.

For every trajectory
\omega_{j}\in\mathcal{D}^{E}
,
ERD
computes the return
G_{j}
of

\omega_{j}
based on the input reward
r
(Line
5
). In
the next lines,
ERD
simply computes the categorical projection of the
mixture of Dirac deltas:

\displaystyle\widehat{\eta}=\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}\Big(\sum_{j}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{D}^{E}|}\delta_{G_{j}}\Big),

where the categorical projection operator
\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}
is defined in Eq.
(
8
).

Algorithm 5

ERD
- Estimate the Return Distribution

Input :

dataset
\mathcal{D}^{E}
, reward
r

// Initialize
\widehat{\eta}
:

for

y

\in

\mathcal{Y}

do

43

\widehat{\eta}(y)\leftarrow 0

44

end for

// Loop over all trajectories in
\mathcal{D}^{E}
:

for

\omega

\in

\mathcal{D}^{E}

do

// Compute return of
\omega=\{s_{1},a_{1},\dotsc,s_{H},a_{H},s_{H+1}\}
:

G\leftarrow\sum_{h=1}^{H}r_{h}(s_{h},a_{h})

// Update estimate
\widehat{\eta}
:

if

G\leq 0

then

45

\widehat{\eta}(0)\leftarrow\widehat{\eta}(0)+1

46

end if

47

else if

G>\lfloor\frac{H}{\epsilon_{0}}\rfloor\epsilon_{0}

then

49

48

\widehat{\eta}(\lfloor\frac{H}{\epsilon_{0}}\rfloor\epsilon_{0})\leftarrow\widehat{\eta}(\lfloor\frac{H}{\epsilon_{0}}\rfloor\epsilon_{0})+1

50

end if

51

else

56

52

L\leftarrow\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}\wedge y<G}y

53

U\leftarrow\min_{y\in\mathcal{Y}\wedge y\geq G}y

54

\widehat{\eta}(L)\leftarrow\widehat{\eta}(L)+\frac{U-G}{U-L}

55

\widehat{\eta}(U)\leftarrow\widehat{\eta}(U)+\frac{G-L}{U-L}

57

end if

59

end for

// Normalize:

\widehat{\eta}\leftarrow\widehat{\eta}/|\mathcal{D}^{E}|

60

Return

\widehat{\eta}

ROLLOUT

ROLLOUT
(Algorithm
61
)
takes in input a Markovian policy
\psi
, a transition model
p
, a reward
r
,
an environment index
i
, and a number of trajectories
K
, to construct the MDP

\mathcal{M}\coloneqq(\mathcal{S}^{i},\mathcal{A}^{i},H,s_{0}^{i},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}r})
obtained from MDP
\mathcal{M}^{i}

by replacing the dynamics and reward
p^{i},r^{i}
with the input
p,r
.

ROLLOUT
collects
K
trajectories by playing policy
\psi
in
\mathcal{M}
for

K
times, computes the return
G
of each trajectory, and then returns a
dataset
\mathcal{D}
containing these
K
returns.
In other words, with abuse of notation, we say that the outputted dataset

\mathcal{D}=\{G_{k}\}_{k\in\llbracket K\rrbracket}
is obtained by collecting
K
samples
G_{k}
from
distribution
\eta^{p,r,\psi}
.

Algorithm 6

ROLLOUT

Input :

policy
\psi
, transition model
p
, reward
r
,
environment index
i
, number of trajectories
K

\mathcal{D}\leftarrow\{\}

// Loop over the number of trajectories:

for

_
\in

\{1,2,\dotsc,K\}

do

s\leftarrow s_{0}^{i}

y\leftarrow 0

/*
y
keeps track of the accumulated reward */

62

for

h=1
to
H

do

66

63

a\leftarrow\psi_{h}(s,y)

64

y\leftarrow y+r_{h}(s,a)

65

s\leftarrow s^{\prime}
where
s^{\prime}\sim p_{h}(\cdot|s,a)

67

end for

69

68

\mathcal{D}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}\cup\{y\}

70

end for

71

Return

\mathcal{D}

E.3
Time and Space Complexities

The time and space complexities of the subroutines are:

•

EXPLORE
:
time
=
\mathcal{O}\Big(N\tau\Big)
for
collecting
\tau
samples from the
N
environments;
space
=

\mathcal{O}\Big(SAHN\Big)
for storing the estimates of the transition model
of the
N
environments.

•

ERD
:
time
=
\mathcal{O}\Big(H\tau^{E}+H/\epsilon_{0}\Big)

for computing the return of each trajectory demontrated by the expert and
initializing an estimate of the return distribution;
space
=

\mathcal{O}\Big(H/\epsilon_{0}\Big)
to store an estimate of the return
distribution.

•

PLANNING
:
time
=
\mathcal{O}\Big(S^{2}AH^{2}/\epsilon_{0}\Big)
for
doing backward induction in the enlarged discretized MDP;
space
=

\mathcal{O}\Big(SAH^{2}/\epsilon_{0}\Big)
to store a Q-function in the enlarged
discretized MDP.

•

ROLLOUT
:
time
=
\mathcal{O}\Big(KH\Big)
for simulating
K

trajectories long
H
;
space
=
\mathcal{O}\Big(K\Big)
for storing the
returns of the
K
trajectories.

Using these complexities, we derive the complexities of
CATY-UL
and
TRACTOR-UL
as:

•

CATY-UL
:
time
=

\mathcal{O}\Big(N\tau+MN\Big(H\tau^{E}+S^{2}AH^{2}/\epsilon_{0}\Big)\Big)
for
calling
EXPLORE
once and then both
ERD
and
PLANNING

MN
times, where
M
denotes the number of input utilities to which
CATY-UL
is
applied;
space
=
\mathcal{O}\Big(SAHN+SAH^{2}/\epsilon_{0}\Big)
where the
dominant terms are for storing a transition model in
EXPLORE
and a
Q-function in
PLANNING
.

•

TRACTOR-UL
:
time
=

\mathcal{O}\Big(N\tau+NH\tau^{E}+T\Big(NS^{2}AH^{2}/\epsilon_{0}+NKH+Q_{time}\Big)\Big)

for calling
EXPLORE
once,
ERD

N
times, both

PLANNING
and
ROLLOUT

TN
times, and executing
T
times the
Euclidean projection onto
\overline{\underline{\mathfrak{U}}}_{L}
using some
optimization solver (
Q_{time}
represents this term);
space
=

\mathcal{O}\Big(NH/\epsilon_{0}+SAH^{2}/\epsilon_{0}+K+Q_{space}\Big)
for storing
the
N
estimates of return distributions, for calling
PLANNING
and

ROLLOUT
, and for executing some optimization solver for Euclidean projection
(
Q_{space}
represents this term).

Observe that the time and space complexities of the proposed algorithms are
polynomial in the amount of data (
\tau,\tau^{E}
), in the number of
environments (
N
), and in the size of the environments (
S,A,H
). Moreover,
both
CATY-UL
and
TRACTOR-UL
have time complexities that grow linearly in the
number of runs (resp.
M
and
T
), and note that the complexity of
TRACTOR-UL
grows linearly also in the number of simulated trajectories (
K
) and in the
complexity of the optimization solver used for the Euclidean projection
(
Q_{time}
). Observe that the complexities depend on
1/\epsilon_{0}
, where

\epsilon_{0}>0
is the discretization parameter.

From a theoretical perspective, if we want that, with probability at least

1-\delta
, the outputs of
CATY-UL
and
TRACTOR-UL
are
\epsilon
-accurate, then,
under the assumption that the output of the optimization solver adopted for the
Euclidean projection is
exact
, Theorems
5.1

and
5.2
show that it suffices to take

\epsilon_{0}=\Theta(\epsilon^{2}/(HN^{2}))
,

\tau^{E}\leq\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\Big(\frac{N^{4}H^{4}}{\epsilon^{4}}\log\frac{1}{\delta}\Big)
,

\tau\leq\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\Big(\frac{N^{2}SAH^{5}}{\epsilon^{2}}\Big(S+\log\frac{1}{\delta}\Big)\Big)
,

T\leq\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{N^{4}H^{4}}{\epsilon^{4}}\Big)
,

K\leq\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\Big(\frac{N^{2}H^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}}\log\frac{1}{\delta}\Big)
,
for obtaining a time and space complexity for the algorithms that grow
polynomially in
S,A,H
,
N,

\frac{1}{\epsilon}
,

\log\frac{1}{\delta},Q_{time},Q_{space}
.

E.4
Analysis of
CATY-UL

See
5.1

Proof.

Observe that the classification carried out by
CATY-UL
complies with the
statement in the theorem as long as we can demonstrate that:

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{P}}\limits_{\{\mathcal{M}^{i}\}_{i},\{\pi^{E,i}\}_{i}}\Big(\sup\limits_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\Big|\sum\limits_{i\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{p^{i},r^{i},\pi^{E,i}}(U)-\sum\limits_{i\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{i}(U)\Big|\leq\epsilon\Big)\geq 1-\delta,

where
\mathbb{P}_{\{\mathcal{M}^{i}\}_{i},\{\pi^{E,i}\}_{i}}
represents the joint
probability distribution induced by the exploration phase of
CATY-UL
and the
execution of each
\pi^{E,i}
in the corresponding
\mathcal{M}^{i}
.

We can rewrite this expression as:

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\Big|\sum\limits_{i\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{p^{i},r^{i},\pi^{E,i}}(U)-\sum\limits_{i\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{i}(U)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\sup\limits_{U\in\mathcal{U}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sum\limits_{i\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}}\Big|\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{p^{i},r^{i},\pi^{E,i}}(U)-\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{i}(U)\Big|

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(1)}}}{\leq}\sum\limits_{i\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sup\limits_{U\in\mathcal{U}}}\Big|\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{p^{i},r^{i},\pi^{E,i}}(U)-\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{i}(U)\Big|,

where at (1) we have upper bounded the maximum of a sum with the sum of the
maxima. This shows that we can obtain the result as long as we can demonstrate
that, for all
i\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
, it holds that:

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{P}}\limits_{p^{i},r^{i},\pi^{E,i}}\Big(\sup\limits_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\Big|\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{p^{i},r^{i},\pi^{E,i}}(U)-\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{i}(U)\Big|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{N}\Big)\geq 1-\frac{\delta}{N};

(15)

the statement of the theorem would then follow from a union bound.
Therefore, let us omit the
i
index for simplicity, and let us try to obtain
the bound in Eq. (
15
). We can write:

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\big|\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{p,r,\pi^{E}}(U)-\widehat{\mathcal{C}}(U)\big|

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sup\limits_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\big|\big(J^{*}(U;p,r)-J^{\pi^{E}}(U;p,r)\big)-\big(\widehat{J}^{*}(U)-\widehat{J}^{E}(U)\big)\big|

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(2)}}}{\leq}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sup\limits_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\big|}J^{\pi^{E}}(U;p,r)-\widehat{J}^{E}(U){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big|}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sup\limits_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\big|}J^{*}(U;p,r)-\widehat{J}^{*}(U){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big|}

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(3)}}}{=}\sup\limits_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\big|\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}}[U(G)]-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\widehat{\eta}^{E}}[U(G)]

\displaystyle\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}})}[U(G)]}\big|+\sup\limits_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\big|J^{*}(U;p,r)-\widehat{J}^{*}(U)\big|

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(4)}}}{\leq}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sup\limits_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\big|}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}}[U(G)]-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}})}[U(G)]{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big|}

\displaystyle\qquad+{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sup\limits_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\big|}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}})}[U(G)]-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\widehat{\eta}^{E}}[U(G)]{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\big|}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sup\limits_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\big|J^{*}(U;p,r)-\widehat{J}^{*}(U)\big|

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(5)}}}{\leq}\sup\limits_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f:\,f\text{ is }L\text{-Lipschitz}}}\big|\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}}[f(G)]-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}})}[f(G)]\big|

\displaystyle\qquad+\sup\limits_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\big|\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}})}[U(G)]-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\widehat{\eta}^{E}}[U(G)]\big|

\displaystyle\qquad+\sup\limits_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\big|J^{*}(U;p,r)-\widehat{J}^{*}(U)\big|

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(6)}}}{=}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L\cdot w_{1}(\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}},\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}))}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sup\limits_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\big|\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}})}[U(G)]-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\widehat{\eta}^{E}}[U(G)]\big|

\displaystyle\qquad\sup\limits_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\big|J^{*}(U;p,r)-\widehat{J}^{*}(U)\big|,

where at (2) we have applied triangle inequality, at (3) we use the definition
of
J^{\pi^{E}}(U;p,r)
, and that of
\widehat{J}^{E}(U)
(Line
1
of
CATY-UL
), and we have added and subtracted a term, where operator

\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}
is defined in Eq. (
8
). We remark
that distribution
\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}
may have a support that grows exponentially
in
H
, while both
\widehat{\eta}^{E}
and

\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}})
are supported on
\mathcal{Y}
. Note that

\widehat{\eta}^{E}
and
\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}})
are different
distributions, since the former is the projection on
\mathcal{Y}
of an
estimate of
\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}
. At (4), we apply triangle inequality, at (5) we
use the hypothesis that all utilities are
L
-Lipschitz
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathfrak{U}_{L}
, and
notice that
\mathfrak{U}_{L}
is a subset of all
L
-Lipschitz functions

f:[0,H]\to[0,H]
, and at (6) we apply the duality formula for the 1-Wasserstein
distance
w_{1}
(see Eq. (6.3) in Chapter 6 of
Villani (2008)
).

Concerning the case
|\mathcal{U}|=1
, we apply, for all
i\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
, Lemma

E.3
with probability
\delta/(2N)
and
accuracy
\epsilon/(3N)
, and Lemma

E.5
with probability
\delta/(2N)
and
accuracy
\epsilon/(3N)
, while
we bound the 1-Wasserstein distance through Lemma
E.1
, to
obtain, through an application of the union bound, that:

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{P}}\limits_{\{\mathcal{M}^{i}\}_{i},\{\pi^{E,i}\}_{i}}\Big(\sup\limits_{U\in\mathcal{U}}

\displaystyle\Big|\sum\limits_{i\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{p^{i},r^{i},\pi^{E,i}}(U)-\sum\limits_{i\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{i}(U)\Big|\leq

\displaystyle\qquad NL\sqrt{2H\epsilon_{0}}+\epsilon/3+NHL\epsilon_{0}+\epsilon/3\Big)\geq 1-\delta,

as long as, for all
i\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
:

\displaystyle\tau^{E,i}\geq\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\bigg(\frac{N^{2}H^{2}\log\frac{N}{\delta}}{\epsilon^{2}}\bigg),

\displaystyle\tau^{i}\geq\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\bigg(\frac{N^{2}SAH^{4}}{\epsilon^{2}}\log\frac{SAHN}{\delta\epsilon_{0}}\bigg).

By setting
\epsilon_{0}=\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{72HL^{2}N^{2}}
, we obtain that:

\displaystyle NL\sqrt{2H\epsilon_{0}}+NHL\epsilon_{0}=\frac{\epsilon}{6}+\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{72LN}\leq\epsilon/3.

By putting this bound into the bound on
\tau^{i}
, we get the result.

When
\mathcal{U}
is an arbitrary subset of
\mathfrak{U}_{L}
, we apply, for all
i\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
, Lemma

E.4
with probability
\delta/(2N)
and
accuracy
\epsilon/(3N)
, and Lemma

E.13
with probability
\delta/(2N)
and
accuracy
\epsilon/(3N)
, while
we bound the 1-Wasserstein distance through Lemma
E.1
, to
obtain, through an application of the union bound, that:

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{P}}\limits_{\{\mathcal{M}^{i}\}_{i},\{\pi^{E,i}\}_{i}}\Big(\sup\limits_{U\in\mathcal{U}}

\displaystyle\Big|\sum\limits_{i\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}\overline{\mathcal{C}}_{p^{i},r^{i},\pi^{E,i}}(U)-\sum\limits_{i\in\llbracket N\rrbracket}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}^{i}(U)\Big|\leq

\displaystyle\qquad NL\sqrt{2H\epsilon_{0}}+\epsilon/3+NHL\epsilon_{0}+\epsilon/3\Big)\geq 1-\delta,

as long as, for all
i\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
:

\displaystyle\tau^{E,i}\geq\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\Big(\frac{N^{2}H^{3}}{\epsilon^{2}\epsilon_{0}}\log\frac{HN}{\delta\epsilon_{0}}\Big),

\displaystyle\tau^{i}\geq\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\Big(\frac{N^{2}SAH^{5}}{\epsilon^{2}}\Big(S+\log\frac{SAHN}{\delta}\Big)\Big).

Again, by setting
\epsilon_{0}=\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{72HL^{2}N^{2}}
, we obtain that:

\displaystyle NL\sqrt{2H\epsilon_{0}}+NHL\epsilon_{0}=\frac{\epsilon}{6}+\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{72LN}\leq\epsilon/3.

By putting this bound into the bounds on
\tau^{E,i}
and
\tau^{i}
, we get the
result.
∎

E.4.1
Lemmas on the Expert’s Return Distribution

Lemma E.1
.

Let the projection operator

\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}
be defined as in Eq. (
8
), over set

\mathcal{Y}
with discretization
\epsilon_{0}
. Then, for all
i\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
, it holds
that:

\displaystyle w_{1}(\eta^{p^{i},r^{i},\pi^{E,i}},\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(\eta^{p^{i},r^{i},\pi^{E,i}}))\leq\sqrt{2H\epsilon_{0}}.

Proof.

For the sake of simplicity, we omit index
i\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
, but the following
derivation can be applied to all the
N
demonstrations.

By applying Lemma 5.2 of
Rowland et al. (2024)
, replacing term

1/(1-\gamma)
with horizon
H
, we get:

\displaystyle w_{1}(\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}},\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}))

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{H}\ell_{2}(\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}},\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}})).

Similarly to the proof of Proposition 3 of

Rowland et al. (2018)
, we can write:

\displaystyle\ell_{2}^{2}(\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}},\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}))

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(1)}}}{\coloneqq}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(F_{\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}}(y)-F_{\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}})}(y))^{2}dy

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(2)}}}{=}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\int_{0}^{H}}(F_{\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}}(y)-F_{\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}})}(y))^{2}dy

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(3)}}}{=}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sum\limits_{j\in\llbracket d-1\rrbracket}\int_{y_{j}}^{y_{j+1}}}(F_{\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}}(y)-F_{\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}})}(y))^{2}dy

\displaystyle\qquad+{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\int_{y_{d}}^{H}}(F_{\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}}(y)-F_{\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}})}(y))^{2}dy

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(4)}}}{\leq}\sum\limits_{j\in\llbracket d-1\rrbracket}\int_{y_{j}}^{y_{j+1}}(F_{\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}}(y)-F_{\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}})}(y))^{2}dy+{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\epsilon_{0}}

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(5)}}}{\leq}\sum\limits_{j\in\llbracket d-1\rrbracket}\int_{y_{j}}^{y_{j+1}}(F_{\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y_{j+1}})-F_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y_{j}}))^{2}dy+\epsilon_{0}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{j\in\llbracket d-1\rrbracket}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(y_{j+1}-y_{j})}(F_{\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}}(y_{j+1})-F_{\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}}(y_{j}))^{2}+\epsilon_{0}

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(6)}}}{=}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\epsilon_{0}}\sum\limits_{j\in\llbracket d-1\rrbracket}(F_{\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}}(y_{j+1})-F_{\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}}(y_{j}))^{2}+\epsilon_{0}

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(7)}}}{\leq}\epsilon_{0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Big(}\sum\limits_{j\in\llbracket d-1\rrbracket}(F_{\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}}(y_{j+1})-F_{\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}}(y_{j}){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Big)^{2}}+\epsilon_{0}

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(8)}}}{=}\epsilon_{0}\big(F_{\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y_{d}})-F_{\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y_{1}})\big)^{2}+\epsilon_{0}

\displaystyle\leq 2\epsilon_{0},

where at (1) we have applied the definition of
\ell_{2}
distance (Eq.
(
7
)), at (2) we recognize that the two
distributions
\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}},\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}})
are
defined on
[0,H]
, at (3) we use the additivity property of the integral,
using notation

\mathcal{Y}\coloneqq\{0,\epsilon_{0},2\epsilon_{0},\dotsc,\lfloor H/\epsilon_{0}\rfloor\epsilon_{0}\}
,

d\coloneqq|\mathcal{Y}|=\lfloor H/\epsilon_{0}\rfloor+1
,
y_{1}\coloneqq 0,y_{2}\coloneqq\epsilon_{0},y_{3}\coloneqq 2\epsilon_{0},\dotsc,y_{d}\coloneqq\lfloor H/\epsilon_{0}\rfloor\epsilon_{0}
, (notation introduced in Section
5
). At (4) we upper bound
\int_{y_{d}}^{H}(F_{\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}}(y)-F_{\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}})}(y))^{2}dy\leq\int_{y_{d}}^{H}dy=H-y_{d}=H-\lfloor H/\epsilon_{0}\rfloor\epsilon_{0}=\epsilon_{0}(H/\epsilon_{0}-\lfloor H/\epsilon_{0}\rfloor)\leq\epsilon_{0}
since the difference of cumulative distribution functions is bounded by 1.
At (5), thanks to the definition of the projection operator

\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}
(Eq. (
8
)), we notice that, for

y\in[y_{j},y_{j+1}]
, it holds that

F_{\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}})}(y)\in[F_{\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}}(y_{j}),F_{\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}}(y_{j+1})]
, thus we can upper bound the integrand
through the maximum, constant, difference of cumulative distribution
functions. At (6) we use the definition of set
\mathcal{Y}
, i.e., an

\epsilon_{0}
-covering of the
[0,H]
interval, at (7) we use the
Cauchy-Schwarz’s inequality
\sum_{j}(x_{j})^{2}\leq(\sum_{j}x_{j})^{2}
for
x_{j}\geq 0
,
and noticed that the summands are always non-negative, at (8) we apply a
telescoping argument.

The result follows by taking the square root of both sides.
∎

Lemma E.2
.

Let
i\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
, and let
f\in[0,H]^{d}
be an arbitrary
d
-dimensional
vector. Denote by

G_{1},G_{2},\dotsc,G_{\tau^{E,i}}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\eta^{p^{i},r^{i},\pi^{E,i}}

the random variables representing the returns of the
\tau^{E,i}
trajectories
inside dataset
\mathcal{D}^{E,i}
. Let
\widehat{\eta}^{E,i}
be the random output of
Algorithm
5
that depends on the random variables

G_{1},G_{2},\dotsc,G_{\tau^{E,i}}
. Then, it holds that:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{G_{1},G_{2},\dotsc,G_{\tau^{E,i}}\sim\eta^{p^{i},r^{i},\pi^{E,i}}}\bigg[\mathbb{E}_{y\sim\widehat{\eta}^{E,i}}\Big[f(y)\Big]\bigg]=\mathbb{E}_{y\sim\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(\eta^{p^{i},r^{i},\pi^{E,i}})}\Big[f(y)\Big].

Proof.

We omit index
i
for simplicity, but the proof can be carried out for all

i\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
independently. To prove the statement, we use the notation
described in Appendix
E.2
for the
Dirac delta, to provide an explicit representation of both the distribution

\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}})
and the “random” distribution

\widehat{\eta}^{E}
.

We consider distribution
\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}
supported on

\mathcal{Z}\coloneqq\{z_{1},z_{2},\dotsc,z_{M}\}\subseteq[0,H]
, while distributions

\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}),\widehat{\eta}^{E}
are supported on set

\mathcal{Y}=\{y_{1},y_{2},\dotsc,y_{d}\}\subseteq[0,H]
.

W.r.t. distribution
\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}})
, we can write:

\displaystyle\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}})

\displaystyle=\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}\Big(\sum\limits_{k\in\llbracket M\rrbracket}\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}(z_{k})\delta_{z_{k}}\Big)

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(1)}}}{=}\sum\limits_{k\in\llbracket M\rrbracket}\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}(z_{k}){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(\delta_{z_{k}})}

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(2)}}}{=}\sum\limits_{k\in\llbracket M\rrbracket}\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}(z_{k}){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Big(\delta_{y_{1}}\mathds{1}\{z_{k}\leq y_{1}\}+\delta_{y_{d}}\mathds{1}\{z_{k}>y_{d}\}}

\displaystyle\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+\sum\limits_{j\in\llbracket d-1\rrbracket}\Big(\frac{y_{j+1}-z_{k}}{y_{j+1}-y_{j}}\delta_{y_{j}}+\frac{z_{k}-y_{j}}{y_{j+1}-y_{j}}\delta_{y_{j+1}}\Big)\mathds{1}\{z_{k}\in(y_{j},y_{j+1}]\}\Big)}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\delta_{y_{1}}}\sum\limits_{k\in\llbracket M\rrbracket}\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}(z_{k})\Big(\mathds{1}\{z_{k}\leq y_{1}\}+\frac{y_{2}-z_{k}}{y_{2}-y_{1}}\mathds{1}\{z_{k}\in(y_{1},y_{2}]\}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum\limits_{j\in\{2,\dotsc,d-1\}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\delta_{y_{j}}}\Big(\sum\limits_{k\in\llbracket M\rrbracket}\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}(z_{k})\Big(\frac{y_{j+1}-z_{k}}{y_{j+1}-y_{j}}\mathds{1}\{z_{k}\in(y_{i},y_{j+1}]\}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{z_{k}-y_{j-1}}{y_{i}-y_{j-1}}\mathds{1}\{z_{k}\in(y_{j-1},y_{i}]\}\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad+{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\delta_{y_{d}}}\sum\limits_{k\in\llbracket M\rrbracket}\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}(z_{k})\Big(\mathds{1}\{z_{k}>y_{d}\}+\frac{z_{k}-y_{d-1}}{y_{d}-y_{d-1}}\mathds{1}\{z_{k}\in(y_{d-1},y_{d}]\}\Big),

where at (1) we have applied the extension in Eq. (
9
) of
the projection operator
\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}
to finite mixtures of Dirac
distributions, and at (2) we have applied its definition (Eq. (
8
)).

Concerning distribution
\widehat{\eta}^{E}
, based on Algorithm
5
,
we can write:

\displaystyle\widehat{\eta}^{E}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\delta_{y_{1}}}}{\tau^{E}}\Big(\sum\limits_{t\in\llbracket\tau^{E}\rrbracket}\Big(\mathds{1}\{G_{t}\leq y_{1}\}+\frac{y_{2}-G_{t}}{y_{2}-y_{1}}\mathds{1}\{G_{t}\in(y_{1},y_{2}]\}\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum\limits_{j\in\{2,\dotsc,d-1\}}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\delta_{y_{j}}}}{\tau^{E}}\Big(\sum\limits_{t\in\llbracket\tau^{E}\rrbracket}\Big(\frac{y_{j+1}-G_{t}}{y_{j+1}-y_{j}}\mathds{1}\{G_{t}\in(y_{i},y_{j+1}]\}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{G_{t}-y_{j-1}}{y_{i}-y_{j-1}}\mathds{1}\{G_{t}\in(y_{j-1},y_{i}]\}\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\delta_{y_{d}}}}{\tau^{E}}\Big(\sum\limits_{t\in\llbracket\tau^{E}\rrbracket}\Big(\mathds{1}\{G_{t}>y_{d}\}+\frac{G_{t}-y_{d-1}}{y_{d}-y_{d-1}}\mathds{1}\{G_{t}\in(y_{d-1},y_{d}]\}\Big)\Big).

Now, if we take the expectation of the random vector
\widehat{\eta}^{E}
w.r.t.

\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}
, we get:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{G_{1},G_{2},\dotsc,G_{\tau^{E}}\sim\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}}\Big[\widehat{\eta}^{E}\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathbb{E}_{G_{1},G_{2},\dotsc,G_{\tau^{E}}\sim\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}}\bigg[\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\delta_{y_{1}}}}{\tau^{E}}\Big(\sum\limits_{t\in\llbracket\tau^{E}\rrbracket}\Big(\mathds{1}\{G_{t}\leq y_{1}\}+\frac{y_{2}-G_{t}}{y_{2}-y_{1}}\mathds{1}\{G_{t}\in(y_{1},y_{2}]\}\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum\limits_{j\in\{2,\dotsc,d-1\}}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\delta_{y_{j}}}}{\tau^{E}}\Big(\sum\limits_{t\in\llbracket\tau^{E}\rrbracket}\Big(\frac{y_{j+1}-G_{t}}{y_{j+1}-y_{j}}\mathds{1}\{G_{t}\in(y_{i},y_{j+1}]\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{G_{t}-y_{j-1}}{y_{i}-y_{j-1}}\mathds{1}\{G_{t}\in(y_{j-1},y_{i}]\}\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\delta_{y_{d}}}}{\tau^{E}}\Big(\sum\limits_{t\in\llbracket\tau^{E}\rrbracket}\Big(\mathds{1}\{G_{t}>y_{d}\}+\frac{G_{t}-y_{d-1}}{y_{d}-y_{d-1}}\mathds{1}\{G_{t}\in(y_{d-1},y_{d}]\}\Big)\Big)\bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(3)}}}{=}\mathbb{E}_{G\sim\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}}\bigg[{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\delta_{y_{1}}}\Big(\mathds{1}\{G\leq y_{1}\}+\frac{y_{2}-G}{y_{2}-y_{1}}\mathds{1}\{G\in(y_{1},y_{2}]\}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum\limits_{j\in\{2,\dotsc,d-1\}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\delta_{y_{j}}}\Big(\frac{y_{j+1}-G}{y_{j+1}-y_{j}}\mathds{1}\{G\in(y_{i},y_{j+1}]\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{G-y_{j-1}}{y_{i}-y_{j-1}}\mathds{1}\{G\in(y_{j-1},y_{i}]\}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\delta_{y_{d}}}\Big(\mathds{1}\{G>y_{d}\}+\frac{G-y_{d-1}}{y_{d}-y_{d-1}}\mathds{1}\{G\in(y_{d-1},y_{d}]\}\Big)\bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(4)}}}{=}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\delta_{y_{1}}}\sum\limits_{k\in\llbracket M\rrbracket}\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}(z_{k})\Big(\mathds{1}\{z_{k}\leq y_{1}\}+\frac{y_{2}-z_{k}}{y_{2}-y_{1}}\mathds{1}\{z_{k}\in(y_{1},y_{2}]\}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum\limits_{j\in\{2,\dotsc,d-1\}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\delta_{y_{j}}}\Big(\sum\limits_{k\in\llbracket M\rrbracket}\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}(z_{k})\Big(\frac{y_{j+1}-z_{k}}{y_{j+1}-y_{j}}\mathds{1}\{z_{k}\in(y_{i},y_{j+1}]\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{z_{k}-y_{j-1}}{y_{i}-y_{j-1}}\mathds{1}\{z_{k}\in(y_{j-1},y_{i}]\}\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\delta_{y_{d}}}\sum\limits_{k\in\llbracket M\rrbracket}\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}(z_{k})\Big(\mathds{1}\{z_{k}>y_{d}\}+\frac{z_{k}-y_{d-1}}{y_{d}-y_{d-1}}\mathds{1}\{z_{k}\in(y_{d-1},y_{d}]\}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(5)}}}{=}\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}),

where at (3) we use the fact that
G_{1},G_{2},\dotsc,G_{\tau^{E}}
are independent
and identically distributed, at (4) we apply the linearity of the expectation,
we notice that
\delta_{y_{j}}
does not depend on
G
for all
j\in\llbracket d\rrbracket
, and
we notice that, for any
y\in\mathcal{Y}
, it holds that
\mathbb{E}_{G\sim\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}}\big[\mathds{1}\{G\leq y\}\big]=\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}(G\leq y)=\sum_{k\in\llbracket M\rrbracket}\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}(z_{k})\mathds{1}\{z_{k}\leq y\}
, where we have
abused notation by writing
\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}(G\leq y)
to mean the probability,
under distribution
\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}
, that event
\{G\leq y\}
happens.
Moreover, similarly, we notice that, for any
y,y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}
, it holds that
\mathbb{E}_{G\sim\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}}\big[G\cdot\mathds{1}\{G\in[y,y^{\prime}]\}\big]=\sum_{k\in\llbracket M\rrbracket}z_{k}\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}(z_{k})\mathds{1}\{z_{k}\in[y,y^{\prime}]\}
. At (5) we simply recognize

\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}})
using the previous expression.

This concludes the proof because the equality of the Dirac delta representations
means that the expectations of any function w.r.t. these two distributions
coincide.
∎

Lemma E.3
.

Let
i\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
and let
\epsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
. If
|\mathcal{U}|=1
, then, with
probability at least
1-\delta
, we have:

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\Big|\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(\eta^{p^{i},r^{i},\pi^{E,i}})}[U(G)]-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\widehat{\eta}^{E,i}}[U(G)]\Big|\leq\epsilon,

as long as:

\displaystyle\tau^{E}\geq c\frac{H^{2}\log\frac{2}{\delta}}{\epsilon^{2}},

where
c
is some positive constant.

Proof.

Let
U
be the only function inside
\mathcal{U}
. Let us omit index
i
for simplicity. Then,
we can write:

\displaystyle\Big|\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\widehat{\eta}^{E}}[U(G)]-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}})}[U(G)]\Big|

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(1)}}}{=}\Big|\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\widehat{\eta}^{E}}[U(G)]-{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}}\Big[}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\widehat{\eta}^{E}}[U(G)]{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Big]}\Big|

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(2)}}}{\leq}cH\sqrt{\frac{\log\frac{2}{\delta}}{\tau^{E}}},

where at (1) we have applied Lemma
E.2
, and at (2) we have applied the Hoeffding’s inequality
noticing that function
U
is bounded in
[0,H]
, and denoting with
c
some positive
constant.

By imposing:

\displaystyle cH\sqrt{\frac{\log\frac{2}{\delta}}{\tau^{E}}}\leq\epsilon,

and solving w.r.t.
\tau^{E}
, we get the result.
∎

Lemma E.4
.

Let
i\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
and let
\epsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
. Then, with probability at least

1-\delta
, we have:

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\Big|\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(\eta^{p^{i},r^{i},\pi^{E,i}})}[U(G)]-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\widehat{\eta}^{E,i}}[U(G)]\Big|\leq\epsilon,

as long as:

\displaystyle\tau^{E}\geq\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\Big(\frac{H^{3}}{\epsilon^{2}\epsilon_{0}}\log\frac{H}{\delta\epsilon_{0}}\Big).

Proof.

Again, let us omit index
i
for simplicity. First, for all possible functions

U\in\mathcal{U}
, we denote by
\overline{U}\in\overline{\mathfrak{U}}_{L}
the function in

\overline{\mathfrak{U}}_{L}
that takes on the values that the function
U
assigns to
the points of set
\mathcal{Y}
. This permits us to write:

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{U\in\mathcal{U}}\Big|\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\widehat{\eta}^{E}}[U(G)]-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}})}[U(G)]\Big|

\displaystyle\qquad=\sup\limits_{\overline{U}\in\overline{\mathfrak{U}}_{L}}\Big|\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\widehat{\eta}^{E}}[\overline{U}(G)]-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}})}[\overline{U}(G)]\Big|

\displaystyle\qquad\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(1)}}}{\leq}\sup\limits_{\overline{U}\in[0,H]^{d}}\Big|\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\widehat{\eta}^{E}}[\overline{U}(G)]-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\text{Proj}_{\mathcal{C}}(\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}})}[\overline{U}(G)]\Big|

\displaystyle\qquad\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(2)}}}{=}\sup\limits_{\overline{U}\in[0,H]^{d}}\Big|\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\widehat{\eta}^{E}}[\overline{U}(G)]-{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}}\Big[}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\widehat{\eta}^{E}}[\overline{U}(G)]{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Big]}\Big|,

where at (1) we upper bound by considering all the possible vectors

\overline{U}\in[0,H]^{d}
, and at (2) we apply Lemma
E.2
.

Now, similarly to the proof of Lemma 7.2 in
Agarwal et al. (2021)
, we
construct an
\epsilon^{\prime}
-covering of set
[0,H]^{d}
, call it
\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon^{\prime}}
,
with
|\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon^{\prime}}|\leq(1+2H\sqrt{d}/\epsilon^{\prime})^{d}
such that, for all

f\in[0,H]^{d}
, there exists
f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon^{\prime}}
for which

\|f-f^{\prime}\|_{2}\leq\epsilon^{\prime}
. By applying a union bound over all

f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon^{\prime}}
and Lemma
E.3
, we
have that, with probability at least
1-\delta
, for all

f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon^{\prime}}
, it holds that:

\displaystyle\Big|\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\widehat{\eta}^{E}}[f^{\prime}(G)]-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}}\Big[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\widehat{\eta}^{E}}[f^{\prime}(G)]\Big]\Big|\leq cH\sqrt{\frac{d\log\frac{2(1+2H\sqrt{d}/\epsilon^{\prime})}{\delta}}{\tau^{E}}}.

(16)

Next, for any
f\in[0,H]^{d}
, denote its closest points (in 2-norm) from

\mathcal{N}_{\epsilon^{\prime}}
as
f^{\prime}
. Then, we have:

\displaystyle\Big|\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\widehat{\eta}^{E}}[f(G)]-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}}\Big[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\widehat{\eta}^{E}}[f(G)]\Big]\Big|

\displaystyle\qquad=\Big|\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\widehat{\eta}^{E}}[f(G)]-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}}\Big[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\widehat{\eta}^{E}}[f(G)]\Big]{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm\Big(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\widehat{\eta}^{E}}[f^{\prime}(G)]-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}}\Big[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\widehat{\eta}^{E}}[f^{\prime}(G)]\Big]\Big)}\Big|

\displaystyle\qquad\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(3)}}}{\leq}\Big|\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\widehat{\eta}^{E}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f^{\prime}}(G)]-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}}\Big[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\widehat{\eta}^{E}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f^{\prime}}(G)]\Big]\Big|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\Big|\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\widehat{\eta}^{E}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f}(G)-{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f^{\prime}}(G)]\Big|+\Big|\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{\eta^{p,r,\pi^{E}}}\Big[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{G\sim\widehat{\eta}^{E}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f}(G)-{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f^{\prime}}(G)]\Big]\Big|

\displaystyle\qquad\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(4)}}}{\leq}cH\sqrt{\frac{d\log\frac{2(1+2H\sqrt{d}/\epsilon^{\prime})}{\delta}}{\tau^{E}}}+2\epsilon^{\prime}

\displaystyle\qquad\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(5)}}}{\leq}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c^{\prime}}H\sqrt{\frac{d\log\frac{Hd\tau^{E}}{\delta}}{\tau^{E}}}

where at (3) we apply triangle inequality, at (4) we apply the result in Eq.
(
16
), and the fact that, by definition of

\epsilon^{\prime}
-covering,
\|f-f^{\prime}\|_{2}\leq\epsilon^{\prime}
entails that

|f(y)-f(y^{\prime})|\leq\epsilon^{\prime}
for all
y\in\mathcal{Y}
; at (5) we set

\epsilon^{\prime}=1/\tau^{E}
, and we simplify.

The result follows by upper bounding
d\leq H/\epsilon_{0}+1
, and then by
setting:

\displaystyle c^{\prime\prime}H\sqrt{\frac{H\log\frac{H\tau^{E}}{\delta\epsilon_{0}}}{\epsilon_{0}\tau^{E}}}\leq\epsilon,

(17)

and solving w.r.t.
\tau^{E}
, and noticing that for all
\tau^{E}
greater than
some constant, we can get rid of the logarithmic terms in
\tau^{E}
.
∎

E.4.2
Lemmas on the Optimal Performance for Single Utility

In this section, we will omit index
i\in\llbracket N\rrbracket
since the following derivations
can be carried out for each
i
.

We denote the arbitrary MDP in
\{\mathcal{M}^{i}\}_{i}
as
\mathcal{M}=(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},H,s_{0},p,r)
,
and its analogous with discretized reward
\overline{r}
, defined at all

(s,a,h)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\llbracket H\rrbracket
as
\overline{r}_{h}(s,a)\coloneqq\Pi_{\mathcal{R}}[r_{h}(s,a)]
, as

\overline{\mathcal{M}}\coloneqq(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},H,s_{0},p,\overline{r})
. We denote the
analogous MDPs with empirical transition model
\widehat{p}
as
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}=(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},H,s_{0},\widehat{p},r)
and
\widehat{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}\coloneqq(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},H,s_{0},\widehat{p},\overline{r})
.

Given any utility
U\in\mathfrak{U}_{L}
, we denote the corresponding RS-MDPs,
respectively, as

\mathcal{M}_{U},\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{U},\widehat{\mathcal{M}}_{U},\widehat{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{U}
. Concerning
the discretized RS-MDPs
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{U}
and
\widehat{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{U}
, we
denote the corresponding enlarged state space MDPs, respectively, as

\mathfrak{E}[\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{U}]=(\{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h}\}_{h},\mathcal{A},H,(s_{0},0),\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})
and

\mathfrak{E}[\widehat{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{U}]=(\{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h}\}_{h},\mathcal{A},H,(s_{0},0),\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r})
, where we decided to define such enlarged
state space MDPs using the state space
\{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h}\}_{h}
considered by
Algorithm
4
(
PLANNING
) instead of, respectively,

\{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{G}^{p,\overline{r}}_{h}\}_{h}
and

\{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{G}^{\widehat{p},\overline{r}}_{h}\}_{h}
. Thus, the transition models

\mathfrak{p}
and
\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}
, from any
h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket
and
(s,y,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h}\times\mathcal{A}
, assign
to the next state
(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h+1}
the probability:
\mathfrak{p}_{h}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime}|s,y,a)\coloneqq p_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)\mathds{1}\{y^{\prime}=y+\overline{r}_{h}(s,a)\}
and

\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}_{h}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime}|s,y,a)\coloneqq\widehat{p}_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)\mathds{1}\{y^{\prime}=y+\overline{r}_{h}(s,a)\}
.
Moreover, the reward function
\mathfrak{r}
, in any
h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket
and
(s,y,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h}\times\mathcal{A}
,
is
\mathfrak{r}_{h}(s,y,a)=0
if
h<H
, and
\mathfrak{r}_{h}(s,y,a)=U(y+\overline{r}_{h}(s,a))
if

h=H
.

We will make extensive use of notation for
V
- and
Q
- functions introduced in
Appendix
B
.

We are now ready to proceed with the analysis. In general, the analysis shares
similarities to that of Theorem 3 of
Wu & Xu (2023)
, but we use
results also from
Azar et al. (2013)
to obtain tighter bounds.

Lemma E.5
.

Let

\epsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
. For any fixed
L
-Lipschitz utility function

U\in\mathfrak{U}_{L}
, it suffices to execute
CATY-UL
with:

\displaystyle\tau\leq\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\Big(\frac{SAH^{4}}{\epsilon^{2}}\log\frac{SAH}{\delta\epsilon_{0}}\Big),

to obtain
\big|J^{*}(U;p,r)-\widehat{J}^{*}(U)\big|\leq HL\epsilon_{0}+\epsilon

w.p.
1-\delta
.

Proof.

For an arbitrary utility
U\in\mathfrak{U}_{L}
, we can write:

\displaystyle|J^{*}(U;p,r)-\widehat{J}^{*}(U)|

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(1)}}}{=}|J^{*}(U;p,r)-\widehat{J}^{*}(U){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm J^{*}(\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})}|

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(2)}}}{\leq}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}|}J^{*}(U;p,r)-J^{*}(\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r}){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}|}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}|}J^{*}(\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})-\widehat{J}^{*}(U){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}|}

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(3)}}}{=}|J^{*}(U;p,r)-J^{*}(\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})|+|J^{*}(\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})-{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}J^{*}(\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r})}|

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(4)}}}{\leq}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}HL\epsilon_{0}}+|J^{*}(\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})-J^{*}(\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r})|

\displaystyle=HL\epsilon_{0}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}|V_{1}^{*}(s_{0},0;\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})-V_{1}^{*}(s_{0},0;\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r})|}

\displaystyle\leq HL\epsilon_{0}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\max\limits_{h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket,(s,y,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h}\times\mathcal{A}}|Q^{*}_{h}(s,y,a;\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})-Q^{*}_{h}(s,y,a;\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r})|}

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(5)}}}{\leq}HL\epsilon_{0}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\epsilon^{\prime}},

where at (1) we add and subtract the optimal expected utility in the enlarged
MDP
\mathfrak{E}[\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{U}]
considered by Algorithm
4
, but
with the true transition model
\mathfrak{p}
. At (2) we apply triangle inequality, at
(3) we recognize that the estimate
\widehat{J}^{*}(U)
used in
CATY-UL
and
outputted by
PLANNING
(Algorithm
4
) is the optimal
expected utility for the discretized problem with estimated dynamics

\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}
, at (4) we use Proposition 3 of
Wu & Xu (2023)
,
since
U
is
L
-Lipschitz, and at (5) we apply Lemma
E.6
to
bound the distance between
Q
-functions.

By setting:

\displaystyle\underbrace{c\sqrt{\frac{H^{3}\log\frac{4SAHd}{\delta}}{n}}}_{\leq\epsilon/3}+\underbrace{cH^{2}\bigg(\frac{\log\frac{16SAHd}{\delta}}{n}\bigg)^{3/4}}_{\leq\epsilon/3}+\underbrace{cH^{3}\frac{\log\frac{16SAHd}{\delta}}{n}}_{\leq\epsilon/3}\leq\epsilon,

and solving w.r.t.
\epsilon
:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}n\geq c^{\prime}\frac{H^{3}\log\frac{4SAHd}{\delta}}{\epsilon^{2}}\\
n\geq c^{\prime\prime}\frac{H^{8/3}\log\frac{16SAHd}{\delta}}{\epsilon^{4/3}}\\
n\geq c^{\prime\prime\prime}\frac{H^{3}\log\frac{16SAHd}{\delta}}{\epsilon}\end{cases}.

Taking the largest bound, we get:

\displaystyle n\geq c\frac{H^{3}\log\frac{16SAHd}{\delta}}{\epsilon^{2}},

for some positive constant
c
. Since
d\leq H/\epsilon_{0}+1
, we can write:

\displaystyle\tau\geq c^{\prime}\frac{SAH^{4}\log\frac{c^{\prime\prime}SAH}{\delta\epsilon_{0}}}{\epsilon^{2}},

for some positive constants
c^{\prime},c^{\prime\prime}
, where we used that
\tau=SAHn
.
∎

The proof of the following lemma is organized in many lemmas, and is based on
the proof of Theorem 1 of
Azar et al. (2013)
.

Lemma E.6
.

For any
\delta\in(0,1)
, we have:

\displaystyle\max\limits_{h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket,(s,y,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h}\times\mathcal{A}}|Q^{*}_{h}(s,y,a;\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})-Q^{*}_{h}(s,y,a;\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r})|\leq\epsilon^{\prime},

w.p. at least
1-\delta
, where
\epsilon^{\prime}
is defined as:

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\prime}\coloneqq c\sqrt{\frac{H^{3}\log\frac{4SAHd}{\delta}}{n}}+cH^{2}\bigg(\frac{\log\frac{16SAHd}{\delta}}{n}\bigg)^{3/4}+cH^{3}\frac{\log\frac{16SAHd}{\delta}}{n},

for some positive constant
c
.

Proof.

We upper bound one side, and then the other. For all the
h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket,(s,y,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h}\times\mathcal{A}
,
it holds that:

\displaystyle Q^{*}_{h}(s,a,y;\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})-Q^{*}_{h}(s,y,a;\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r})

\displaystyle\qquad\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(1)}}}{\leq}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r},\psi^{*}}\bigg[\sum\limits_{h^{\prime}=h}^{H}\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}\Big(p_{h^{\prime}}(s^{\prime}|s_{h^{\prime}},a_{h^{\prime}})-\widehat{p}_{h^{\prime}}(s^{\prime}|s_{h^{\prime}},a_{h^{\prime}})\Big)V_{h^{\prime}+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y_{h^{\prime}+1};\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\Big|\,s_{h}=s,y_{h}=y,a_{h}=a\bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(2)}}}{\leq}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r},\psi^{*}}\bigg[\sum\limits_{h^{\prime}=h}^{H}c\sqrt{\frac{c_{1}\mathbb{V}_{s^{\prime}\sim\widehat{p}_{h^{\prime}}(\cdot|s_{h^{\prime}},a_{h^{\prime}})}[V^{\psi^{*}}_{h^{\prime}+1}(s^{\prime},y_{h^{\prime}+1};\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r})]}{n}}+b_{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\Big|\,s_{h}=s,y_{h}=y,a_{h}=a\bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c\sqrt{\frac{c_{1}}{n}}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r},\psi^{*}}\bigg[\sum\limits_{h^{\prime}=h}^{H}\sqrt{\mathbb{V}_{s^{\prime}\sim\widehat{p}_{h^{\prime}}(\cdot|s_{h^{\prime}},a_{h^{\prime}})}[V^{\psi^{*}}_{h^{\prime}+1}(s^{\prime},y_{h^{\prime}+1};\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r})]}\,\Big|\,s_{h}=s,y_{h}=y,a_{h}=a\bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}Hb_{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(3)}}}{\leq}c\sqrt{\frac{c_{1}}{n}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sqrt{H^{3}}}+Hb_{2}

\displaystyle\qquad=c\sqrt{\frac{H^{3}\log\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}4}SAHY}{\delta}}{n}}+c^{\prime}H^{2}\bigg(\frac{\log\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}16}SAHY}{\delta}}{n}\bigg)^{3/4}+c^{\prime\prime}H^{3}\frac{\log\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}16}SAHY}{\delta}}{n}

\displaystyle\qquad\eqqcolon\epsilon^{\prime},

where at (1) we have applied Lemma
E.7
, at (2) we have
applied Lemma
E.10
with
\delta/2
of probability, at (3) we have applied Lemma
E.12
.

The proof for the other side of inequality is completely analogous, and it
holds w.p.
1-\delta/2
. The result follows through the application of a union bound.
∎

Lemma E.7
.

For any tuple
h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket,(s,y,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h}\times\mathcal{A}
, it holds that:

\displaystyle Q^{*}_{h}(s,y,a;\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})-Q^{*}_{h}(s,y,a;\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r})\leq\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r},\psi^{*}}\bigg[\sum\limits_{h^{\prime}=h}^{H}\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}

\displaystyle\qquad\Big(p_{h^{\prime}}(s^{\prime}|s_{h^{\prime}},a_{h^{\prime}})-\widehat{p}_{h^{\prime}}(s^{\prime}|s_{h^{\prime}},a_{h^{\prime}})\Big)V_{h^{\prime}+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y_{h^{\prime}+1};\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})\,\Big|\,s_{h}=s,y_{h}=y,a_{h}=a\bigg],

\displaystyle Q^{*}_{h}(s,y,a;\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})-Q^{*}_{h}(s,y,a;\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r})\geq\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r},\widehat{\psi}^{*}}\bigg[\sum\limits_{h^{\prime}=h}^{H}\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}

\displaystyle\qquad\Big(p_{h^{\prime}}(s^{\prime}|s_{h^{\prime}},a_{h^{\prime}})-\widehat{p}_{h^{\prime}}(s^{\prime}|s_{h^{\prime}},a_{h^{\prime}})\Big)V_{h^{\prime}+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y_{h^{\prime}+1};\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})\,\Big|\,s_{h}=s,y_{h}=y,a_{h}=a\bigg],

where
\psi^{*},\widehat{\psi}^{*}
are the optimal policies respectively in
problems
\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r}
and
\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r}
.

Proof.

For any
h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket,(s,y,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h}\times\mathcal{A}
, we can write:

\displaystyle Q^{*}_{h}(s,y,a;\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})-Q^{*}_{h}(s,y,a;\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r})

\displaystyle\qquad=Q^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\psi^{*}}}_{h}(s,y,a;\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})-Q^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\widehat{\psi}^{*}}}_{h}(s,y,a;\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r})

\displaystyle\qquad\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(1)}}}{\leq}Q^{\psi^{*}}_{h}(s,y,a;\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})-Q^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\psi^{*}}}_{h}(s,y,a;\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r})

\displaystyle\qquad\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(2)}}}{=}\mathfrak{r}_{h}(s,y,a)+\sum\limits_{(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h+1}}\mathfrak{p}_{h}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime}|s,y,a)V_{h+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime};\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\Big(\mathfrak{r}_{h}(s,y,a)+\sum\limits_{(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h+1}}\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}_{h}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime}|s,y,a)V_{h+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime};\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r})\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(3)}}}{=}\sum\limits_{(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h+1}}\mathfrak{p}_{h}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime}|s,y,a)V_{h+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime};\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\sum\limits_{(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h+1}}\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}_{h}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime}|s,y,a)V_{h+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime};\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm\sum\limits_{(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h+1}}\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}_{h}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime}|s,y,a)V_{h+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime};\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})}

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum\limits_{(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h+1}}\Big(\mathfrak{p}_{h}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime}|s,y,a)-\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}_{h}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime}|s,y,a)\Big)V_{h+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime};\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum\limits_{(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h+1}}\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}_{h}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime}|s,y,a)\Big(V_{h+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime};\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})-V_{h+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime};\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r})\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(4)}}}{=}\sum\limits_{(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h+1}}\Big({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)\mathds{1}\{y+\overline{r}_{h}(s,a)=y^{\prime}\}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\widehat{p}_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)\mathds{1}\{y+\overline{r}_{h}(s,a)=y^{\prime}\}}\Big)V_{h+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime};\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum\limits_{(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h+1}}\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}_{h}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime}|s,y,a)\Big(V_{h+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime};\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})-V_{h+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime};\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r})\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(5)}}}{=}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}}\Big(p_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)-\widehat{p}_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)\Big){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sum\limits_{y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}_{h+1}}}\mathds{1}\{y+\overline{r}_{h}(s,a)=y^{\prime}\}V_{h+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime};\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum\limits_{(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h+1}}\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}_{h}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime}|s,y,a)\Big(V_{h+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime};\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})-V_{h+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime};\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r})\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(6)}}}{=}\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}\Big(p_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)-\widehat{p}_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)\Big){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}V_{h+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y+\overline{r}_{h}(s,a);\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum\limits_{(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h+1}}\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}_{h}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime}|s,y,a)\Big(V_{h+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime};\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})-V_{h+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime};\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r})\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}\Big(p_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)-\widehat{p}_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)\Big)V_{h+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y+\overline{r}_{h}(s,a);\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum\limits_{(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h+1}}\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}_{h}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime}|s,y,a)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\cdot\Big({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}Q}_{h+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\psi^{*}_{h+1}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})};\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})-{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}Q}_{h+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\psi^{*}_{h+1}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})};\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r})\Big),

where at (1) we have used that
\widehat{\psi}^{*}
is the optimal policy in

\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r}
, and thus
Q^{\psi^{*}}_{h}(s,a;\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r})\leq Q^{\widehat{\psi}^{*}}_{h}(s,a;\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r})
.
At (2) we apply the Bellman equation, at (3) we add and subtract the expected
under
\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}
optimal value function under
\mathfrak{p}
, at (4) we use the
definition of transition model
\mathfrak{p},\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}
, at (5) we split the
summations, at (6) we recognize that the indicator function takes on value
1

only when
y+\overline{r}_{h}(s,a)=y^{\prime}
. Finally, we unfold the recursion to obtain the result.

Concerning the second equation, for any
h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket,(s,y,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h}\times\mathcal{A}
, we can write:

\displaystyle Q^{*}_{h}(s,y,a;\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})-Q^{*}_{h}(s,y,a;\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r})

\displaystyle\qquad=Q^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\psi^{*}}}_{h}(s,y,a;\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})-Q^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\widehat{\psi}^{*}}}_{h}(s,y,a;\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r})

\displaystyle\qquad\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(7)}}}{=}\mathfrak{r}_{h}(s,y,a)+\sum\limits_{(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h+1}}\mathfrak{p}_{h}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime}|s,y,a)V_{h+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime};\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\Big(\mathfrak{r}_{h}(s,y,a)+\sum\limits_{(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h+1}}\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}_{h}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime}|s,y,a)V_{h+1}^{\widehat{\psi}^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime};\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r})\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(8)}}}{=}\sum\limits_{(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h+1}}\mathfrak{p}_{h}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime}|s,y,a)V_{h+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime};\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\sum\limits_{(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h+1}}\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}_{h}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime}|s,y,a)V_{h+1}^{\widehat{\psi}^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime};\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pm\sum\limits_{(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h+1}}\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}_{h}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime}|s,y,a)V_{h+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime};\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})}

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum\limits_{(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h+1}}\Big(\mathfrak{p}_{h}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime}|s,y,a)-\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}_{h}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime}|s,y,a)\Big)V_{h+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime};\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum\limits_{(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h+1}}\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}_{h}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime}|s,y,a)\Big(V_{h+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime};\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})-V_{h+1}^{\widehat{\psi}^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime};\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r})\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum\limits_{(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h+1}}\Big({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)\mathds{1}\{y+\overline{r}_{h}(s,a)=y^{\prime}\}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\widehat{p}_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)\mathds{1}\{y+\overline{r}_{h}(s,a)=y^{\prime}\}}\Big)V_{h+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime};\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum\limits_{(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h+1}}\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}_{h}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime}|s,y,a)\Big(V_{h+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime};\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})-V_{h+1}^{\widehat{\psi}^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime};\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r})\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}}\Big(p_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)-\widehat{p}_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)\Big){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sum\limits_{y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}_{h+1}}}\mathds{1}\{y+\overline{r}_{h}(s,a)=y^{\prime}\}V_{h+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime};\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum\limits_{(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h+1}}\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}_{h}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime}|s,y,a)\Big(V_{h+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime};\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})-V_{h+1}^{\widehat{\psi}^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime};\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r})\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}\Big(p_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)-\widehat{p}_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)\Big){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}V_{h+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y+\overline{r}_{h}(s,a);\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum\limits_{(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h+1}}\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}_{h}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime}|s,y,a)\Big(V_{h+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime};\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})-V_{h+1}^{\widehat{\psi}^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime};\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r})\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\overset{\textup{\makebox[0.0pt]{(9)}}}{\geq}\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}\Big(p_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)-\widehat{p}_{h}(s^{\prime}|s,a)\Big)V_{h+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y+\overline{r}_{h}(s,a);\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum\limits_{(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h+1}}\widehat{\mathfrak{p}}_{h}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime}|s,y,a)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\cdot\Big({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}Q}_{h+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\widehat{\psi}^{*}_{h+1}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})};\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})-{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}Q}_{h+1}^{\widehat{\psi}^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime},\widehat{\psi}^{*}_{h+1}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime});\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r})\Big),

where at (7) we have applied the Bellman equation, at (8) we have added and
subtracted a term, and at (9) we have used that

V_{h+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime};\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})=Q_{h+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime},\psi^{*}_{h+1}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime});\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})\geq Q_{h+1}^{\psi^{*}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime},\widehat{\psi}^{*}_{h+1}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime});\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})
, since
\psi^{*}_{h+1}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})
is
the optimal action under
\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r}
, and so, it cannot be worse than action

\widehat{\psi}^{*}_{h+1}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})
. By unfolding the recursion, we obtain the result.
∎

Lemma E.8
.

For any
\delta\in(0,1)
, w.p. at least
1-\delta
, it holds that:

\displaystyle\max_{h\in\llbracket H\rrbracket,(s,y)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{Y}_{h}}|V_{h}^{*}(s,y;\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{r})-V^{\psi^{*}}_{h}(s,y;\widehat{\mathfrak{p}},\mathfrak{r})|
